Psychomotor Agitation: Purposeless non-goal-directed activity, such as pacing or hand-wringing, driven by an inability to sit still.\n  Duration: 1 year and 2 months\n▸ delusions_and_hallucinations\n  - Persecutory Delusions: The false belief that one is being harmed or monitored by other people or organizations.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Erotomanic Delusions: The false belief that another person is in love with or has affection for them.\n  - Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - Somatic Delusions: The baseless conviction that there is a serious abnormality with one's body or organs.\n  - Jealous Delusions: The conviction that one's spouse or romantic partner is being unfaithful without sufficient evidence.\n  - Auditory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of hearing voices or sounds when there is no actual sound.\n  - Visual Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of seeing people or objects that do not exist.\n  - Tactile Hallucinations: Experiencing the sensation of being touched or movement on the skin when there is no actual stimulus.\n  - Olfactory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of smelling odors that do not exist.\n  - Gustatory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of tasting flavors that are not actually present.\n▸ Critical Diagnostic Criteria\n  - However, the fact that the patient has experienced these symptoms for an extended period, with very few moments of stability, and the psychotic symptoms only occur during mood episodes, keeps both Schizoaffective Disorder (Bipolar Type) and Bipolar I Disorder with Psychotic Features as potential diagnoses. The key distinction between these two conditions - whether the psychotic symptoms occur independently of mood episodes for at least two weeks - has not been met according to the patient's account, which is why Bipolar I Disorder with Psychotic Features remains a candidate. When my mood is stable or normal, those things never happen.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate what percentage of the time you've spent in these abnormal mood episodes over the past year and two months, versus times when your mood was relatively stable or normal?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure of an exact percentage, but it feels like these high-energy, fast-paced moods have been happening almost constantly over the last year and two months. There have been very few moments where I felt truly normal or stable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks where you experienced significant psychotic symptoms, such as hallucinations or delusions, without a major mood episode, or have the psychotic symptoms always occurred in conjunction with your mood episodes?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced those hallucinations or delusions without the mood episodes. They are always tied directly to my high-energy or irritable states. I've never had a time where I heard or saw things when my mood was normal or stable.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the times when you feel furious or irritable - are these episodes also accompanied by depressive symptoms, such as feelings of sadness, hopelessness, or loss of interest in activities?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience those depressive feelings like sadness or hopelessness at all. When I get furious, it's just pure anger and frustration, not feeling down or losing interest in things. My energy stays really high even when I'm angry."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["Schizoaffective Disorder (Bipolar Type)", "Bipolar I Disorder with Psychotic Features"], "note": "The patient's description of continuous episodes of high energy and irritability, accompanied by psychotic symptoms such as hearing voices and seeing things that aren't there, suggests a strong connection between the mood episodes and the psychotic symptoms. The fact that the psychotic symptoms only occur during the high-energy or irritable phases and not during periods of normal or stable mood supports the diagnosis of either Schizoaffective Disorder (Bipolar Type) or Bipolar I Disorder with Psychotic Features. The absence of depressive symptoms during the irritable episodes and the continuous nature of the symptoms over the past year and two months do not rule out either diagnosis, as both conditions can present with predominantly manic or mixed episodes. Further evaluation is needed to determine the exact nature and duration of the mood episodes and psychotic symptoms to differentiate between these two diagnoses.", "is_final": false}

I haven't really had a break from this cycle of high energy and unusual experiences during this entire time.\n\nDoctor: Do the breaks from reality, such as hearing voices or seeing things, ever occur without the high energy or irritability, or are they always tied to those mood episodes?\n\nPatient: They are always tied to those mood episodes. I only ever experience those voices and visions when I am in that high-energy or irritable state. When my mood is stable or normal, those things never happen.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate what percentage of the time you've spent in these abnormal mood episodes over the past year and two months, versus times when your mood was relatively stable or normal?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure of an exact percentage, but it feels like these high-energy, fast-paced moods have been happening almost constantly over the last year and two months. There have been very few moments where I felt truly normal or stable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks where you experienced significant psychotic symptoms, such as hallucinations or delusions, without a major mood episode, or have the psychotic symptoms always occurred in conjunction with your mood episodes?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced those hallucinations or delusions without the mood episodes. They are always tied directly to my high-energy or irritable states. I've never had a time where I heard or saw things when my mood was normal or stable.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the times when you feel furious or irritable - are these episodes also accompanied by depressive symptoms, such as feelings of sadness, hopelessness, or loss of interest in activities?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience those depressive feelings like sadness or hopelessness at all. When I get furious, it's just pure anger and frustration, not feeling down or losing interest in things. My energy stays really high even when I'm angry.\n\nDoctor: Are your episodes of high energy and irritability distinct and separate from each other, or do they sometimes blend together or switch rapidly back and forth?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
They definitely blend together and switch back and forth incredibly fast. I can go from feeling completely ecstatic and full of energy to suddenly being furious at the slightest thing in a matter of seconds. It's a very rapid, unpredictable cycle where they often happen at the same time.

    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm here because my mood has been completely out of control for about a year and two months now. I have this overflowing energy where I feel incredibly high and happy, but then I suddenly get extremely irritable and angry over the smallest things. It's like I'm constantly shifting between being on top of the world and being furious.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes of high energy or irritability, have you ever experienced any break from reality, such as hearing voices or seeing things that aren't there?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do hear voices and see things that aren't actually there, which can be really terrifying. However, this only happens when I'm in those super high-energy or really irritable phases. When my mood is normal or flat, I never experience those things at all.\n\nDoctor: How long do these episodes of high energy or irritability, accompanied by the break from reality, typically last?\n\nPatient: These episodes have actually been going on continuously for about a year and two months now. I haven't really had a break from this cycle of high energy and unusual experiences during this entire time.\n\nDoctor: Do the breaks from reality, such as hearing voices or seeing things, ever occur without the high energy or irritability, or are they always tied to those mood episodes?\n\nPatient: They are always tied to those mood episodes. I only ever experience those voices and visions when I am in that high-energy or irritable state. When my mood is stable or normal, those things never happen.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate what percentage of the time you've spent in these abnormal mood episodes over the past year and two months, versus times when your mood was relatively stable or normal?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure of an exact percentage, but it feels like these high-energy, fast-paced moods have been happening almost constantly over the last year and two months. There have been very few moments where I felt truly normal or stable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks where you experienced significant psychotic symptoms, such as hallucinations or delusions, without a major mood episode, or have the psychotic symptoms always occurred in conjunction with your mood episodes?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced those hallucinations or delusions without the mood episodes. They are always tied directly to my high-energy or irritable states. I've never had a time where I heard or saw things when my mood was normal or stable.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the times when you feel furious or irritable - are these episodes also accompanied by depressive symptoms, such as feelings of sadness, hopelessness, or loss of interest in activities?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience those depressive feelings like sadness or hopelessness at all. When I get furious, it's just pure anger and frustration, not feeling down or losing interest in things. My energy stays really high even when I'm angry.\n\nDoctor: Are your episodes of high energy and irritability distinct and separate from each other, or do they sometimes blend together or switch rapidly back and forth?\n\nPatient: They definitely blend together and switch back and forth incredibly fast. I can go from feeling completely ecstatic and full of energy to suddenly being furious at the slightest thing in a matter of seconds. It's a very rapid, unpredictable cycle where they often happen at the same time.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe any patterns or triggers that you've noticed for these rapid mood shifts, or if there are any specific events, people, or situations that tend to precede or follow these episodes?"

    "content": "Previous Candidates:\n[\"Schizoaffective Disorder (Bipolar Type)\", \"Bipolar I Disorder with Psychotic Features\"]\n\nInterview Transcript:\n\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm here because my mood has been completely out of control for about a year and two months now. I have this overflowing energy where I feel incredibly high and happy, but then I suddenly get extremely irritable and angry over the smallest things. It's like I'm constantly shifting between being on top of the world and being furious.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes of high energy or irritability, have you ever experienced any break from reality, such as hearing voices or seeing things that aren't there?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do hear voices and see things that aren't actually there, which can be really terrifying. However, this only happens when I'm in those super high-energy or really irritable phases. When my mood is normal or flat, I never experience those things at all.\n\nDoctor: How long do these episodes of high energy or irritability, accompanied by the break from reality, typically last?\n\nPatient: These episodes have actually been going on continuously for about a year and two months now. I haven't really had a break from this cycle of high energy and unusual experiences during this entire time.\n\nDoctor: Do the breaks from reality, such as hearing voices or seeing things, ever occur without the high energy or irritability, or are they always tied to those mood episodes?\n\nPatient: They are always tied to those mood episodes. I only ever experience those voices and visions when I am in that high-energy or irritable state. When my mood is stable or normal, those things never happen.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate what percentage of the time you've spent in these abnormal mood episodes over the past year and two months, versus times when your mood was relatively stable or normal?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure of an exact percentage, but it feels like these high-energy, fast-paced moods have been happening almost constantly over the last year and two months. There have been very few moments where I felt truly normal or stable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks where you experienced significant psychotic symptoms, such as hallucinations or delusions, without a major mood episode, or have the psychotic symptoms always occurred in conjunction with your mood episodes?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced those hallucinations or delusions without the mood episodes. They are always tied directly to my high-energy or irritable states. I've never had a time where I heard or saw things when my mood was normal or stable.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the times when you feel furious or irritable - are these episodes also accompanied by depressive symptoms, such as feelings of sadness, hopelessness, or loss of interest in activities?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience those depressive feelings like sadness or hopelessness at all. When I get furious, it's just pure anger and frustration, not feeling down or losing interest in things. It's a very rapid, unpredictable cycle where they often happen at the same time.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe any patterns or triggers that you've noticed for these rapid mood shifts, or if there are any specific events, people, or situations that tend to precede or follow these episodes?\n\nPatient: There aren't any specific triggers, events, or people that cause these shifts.
